People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Um… I guess I came because I can’t really make myself go near the front windows anymore. Or that part of the street outside. At work I keep making excuses to stay in the back, like I’ll say I need to organize stock or check boxes, which I mean, there’s always boxes in retail, so it doesn’t sound that weird… but it’s not really about the boxes. It’s just that being up front feels… I don’t know, I just can’t do it, and it’s part of my job, so it’s becoming a problem.

I mean… not one thing exactly, like not a person standing there or anything. It’s just that area. The front windows, and that stretch of street right outside them, I just… I can’t make myself go over there anymore.

Which, honestly, the stockroom is always a mess anyway—my manager keeps saying we need a better system, and nobody puts sizes where they go—but… yeah, I know that’s not really why I’m doing it. Being up front is part of my job, and I’m avoiding it.\n\nDoctor: Is there something that you think might happen to you if you were to go near the front windows or that part of the street?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I can explain it right. It’s becoming a problem because that’s part of my job.\n\nDoctor: Do you think something bad would happen to you if you went near the front windows or that part of the street?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I think it in words like that, like “something bad is going to happen.” It’s more like I just… can’t go there. The front windows, that part of the street, I start looking for any excuse to stay in the back.\n\nLike I’ll suddenly decide the stockroom needs organizing, which it always does, honestly, because people just shove hangers anywhere, and then I can spend twenty minutes pretending that’s the urgent thing. Not specific, no… I mean, that’s kind of what makes me feel ridiculous when I try to explain it. I can’t point to one thing and say, “that’s what I’m scared of.” It’s just the front windows and that part of the street. I came in because I can’t make myself go near them anymore.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… I think so. I mean, even if it wasn’t for work, I don’t think I could just casually stand there or walk by that part of the street. It’s not really about the job itself, I guess.

At work it’s just obvious because I keep making excuses to stay in the back—like, suddenly the back room is my whole life, and there’s always some pile of clothes or boxes or whatever to mess with. But the real thing is I came in because I can’t make myself go near those front windows or that spot on the street anymore. Even if I didn’t work there, I think I’d still want to avoid it.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Um… I guess I came because I can’t really make myself go near the front windows anymore. Or that part of the street outside. It’s just that being up front feels… I don’t know, I just can’t do it, and it’s part of my job, so it’s becoming a problem.\n\nDoctor: Is there something specific outside the front windows or on that part of the street that you're afraid of?\n\nPatient: I mean… not one thing exactly, like not a person standing there or anything. Yeah… I think it’s more that place itself, if that makes sense. Like the front windows and that stretch of street just feel like… I don’t know, like I hit a wall.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… even when I’m not there, if I just think about the front windows or that part of the street, I get uncomfortable. Like, I’ll be at home or on the bus or something and it pops into my head, and then I start thinking about how I’m going to avoid it the next shift.

And then I end up making these stupid little plans, like, okay, I’ll stay in the back and do returns, or I’ll sort boxes, or I’ll say I need to organize the stockroom. Which, I mean, those things do need doing—retail is kind of endless that way—but I know I’m really just trying not to go near the front. It’s part of my job, so it’s not like I can just never go there, but lately I kind of… can’t make myself.

It’s just that being up front feels… I don’t know, I just can’t do it, and it’s part of my job, so it’s becoming a problem.\n\nDoctor: Is there something specific outside the front windows or on that part of the street that you're afraid of?\n\nPatient: I mean… not one thing exactly, like not a person standing there or anything. It’s just that area. The front windows, and that stretch of street right outside them, I just… I can’t make myself go over there anymore.\n\nAt work I keep finding reasons to stay in the back, like saying I need to sort inventory or look for something in the stockroom. Which, honestly, the stockroom is always a mess anyway—my manager keeps saying we need a better system, and nobody puts sizes where they go—but… yeah, I know that’s not really why I’m doing it. Being up front is part of my job, and I’m avoiding it.\n\nDoctor: Is there something that you think might happen to you if you were to go near the front windows or that part of the street?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I can explain it right. The front windows, that part of the street, I start looking for any excuse to stay in the back.\n\nLike I’ll suddenly decide the stockroom needs organizing, which it always does, honestly, because people just shove hangers anywhere, and then I can spend twenty minutes pretending that’s the urgent thing. I mean, even if it wasn’t for work, I don’t think I could just casually stand there or walk by that part of the street. I think… yeah, I probably still would. Like, if someone was with me, maybe I’d feel a little less weird for a second, because I could talk or pretend we were just doing normal stuff, you know? But I don’t think it would really fix it.

I mean, at work there are people around all the time—coworkers, customers, whatever—and I still keep finding reasons to stay in the back. I’ll suddenly be like, “Oh, I should sort these boxes,” or “I need to check the stockroom,” and then an hour goes by. And honestly, that’s part of why I came in, because I can’t make myself go near those front windows or that part of the street anymore, even though I’m supposed to for my job.

Sorry, I’m kind of going in circles. But yeah… being with someone might help a little, but I’d still want to avoid it.

The front windows, that part of the street, I start looking for any excuse to stay in the back.\n\nLike I’ll suddenly decide the stockroom needs organizing, which it always does, honestly, because people just shove hangers anywhere, and then I can spend twenty minutes pretending that’s the urgent thing. But really I know I’m avoiding the front. And it’s a problem because being up there is literally part of my job.\n\nDoctor: Is there something specific that you're afraid of when you think about being near the front windows or that part of the street?\n\nPatient: Not specific, no… I mean, that’s kind of what makes me feel ridiculous when I try to explain it. I came in because I can’t make myself go near them anymore.\n\nAt work I’ll keep finding excuses to stay in the back, like there’s always something to do back there anyway—boxes, returns, clothes people shoved in the wrong place—but I know I’m using it as a reason not to go up front. Which, I mean, those things do need doing—retail is kind of endless that way—but I know I’m really just trying not to go near the front. It’s part of my job, so it’s not like I can just never go there, but lately I kind of… can’t make myself.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still feel the need to avoid the front windows or that part of the street if you weren't alone?\n\nPatient: I think… yeah, I probably still would. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I don’t know if I think something would happen, exactly. It’s not like I have a clear thought like, “If I stand there, this specific thing will happen.” It’s more… I just can’t make myself go there.

Like even before my shift I’ll be thinking, okay, maybe I can just stay in the back and do the boxes, or fix the hangers, or say the stockroom needs sorting. And honestly it always does need sorting, so it’s easy to kind of hide behind that. My manager probably thinks I’m just being helpful or avoiding customers or something, but it’s not that.

I came in because I can’t go near the front windows or that part of the street anymore, not like I’m supposed to. It’s part of my job, and I keep making excuses to stay in the back instead. So… I don’t know if I’d say I’m sure something would happen, but my body acts like I need to stay away.
=====================================

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n10\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF40.2, F43.1\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Um… I guess I came because I can’t really make myself go near the front windows anymore. Or that part of the street outside. At work I keep making excuses to stay in the back, like I’ll say I need to organize stock or check boxes, which I mean, there’s always boxes in retail, so it doesn’t sound that weird… but it’s not really about the boxes. It’s just that being up front feels… I don’t know, I just can’t do it, and it’s part of my job, so it’s becoming a problem.\n\nDoctor: Is there something specific outside the front windows or on that part of the street that you're afraid of?\n\nPatient: I mean… not one thing exactly, like not a person standing there or anything. It’s just that area. The front windows, and that stretch of street right outside them, I just… I can’t make myself go over there anymore.\n\nAt work I keep finding reasons to stay in the back, like saying I need to sort inventory or look for something in the stockroom. Which, honestly, the stockroom is always a mess anyway—my manager keeps saying we need a better system, and nobody puts sizes where they go—but… yeah, I know that’s not really why I’m doing it. Being up front is part of my job, and I’m avoiding it.\n\nDoctor: Is there something that you think might happen to you if you were to go near the front windows or that part of the street?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I can explain it right. It’s not like I have a whole clear picture of, “this exact thing will happen.” It’s more like… if I go near the front windows or that part of the street, my whole body just says no. So I end up staying in the back and making up reasons, like inventory or boxes or whatever.\n\nAnd the stupid thing is, I’m actually good with customers usually. Like I can fold a whole table of shirts while talking to someone about sizes, but now if I’m supposed to be up front, I just start looking for a reason not to be there. It’s becoming a problem because that’s part of my job.\n\nDoctor: Do you think something bad would happen to you if you went near the front windows or that part of the street?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I think it in words like that, like “something bad is going to happen.” It’s more like I just… can’t go there. The front windows, that part of the street, I start looking for any excuse to stay in the back.\n\nLike I’ll suddenly decide the stockroom needs organizing, which it always does, honestly, because people just shove hangers anywhere, and then I can spend twenty minutes pretending that’s the urgent thing. But really I know I’m avoiding the front. And it’s a problem because being up there is literally part of my job.\n\nDoctor: Is there something specific that you're afraid of when you think about being near the front windows or that part of the street?\n\nPatient: Not specific, no… I mean, that’s kind of what makes me feel ridiculous when I try to explain it. I can’t point to one thing and say, “that’s what I’m scared of.” It’s just the front windows and that part of the street. I came in because I can’t make myself go near them anymore.\n\nAt work I’ll keep finding excuses to stay in the back, like there’s always something to do back there anyway—boxes, returns, clothes people shoved in the wrong place—but I know I’m using it as a reason not to go up front. And being near the windows is part of my job, so… yeah, it’s becoming a real problem.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would feel anxious or uncomfortable if you were near the front windows or that part of the street for a reason unrelated to your job?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, even if it wasn’t for work, I don’t think I could just casually stand there or walk by that part of the street. It’s not really about the job itself, I guess.\n\nAt work it’s just obvious because I keep making excuses to stay in the back—like, suddenly the back room is my whole life, and there’s always some pile of clothes or boxes or whatever to mess with. But the real thing is I came in because I can’t make myself go near those front windows or that spot on the street anymore. Even if I didn’t work there, I think I’d still want to avoid it.\n\nDoctor: Is there something about the front windows or that part of the street itself that you're trying to avoid, rather than what might happen there?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think it’s more that place itself, if that makes sense. Like the front windows and that stretch of street just feel like… I don’t know, like I hit a wall.\n\nI keep telling myself I’m just busy in the back, because there really is always stuff back there—someone leaves boxes half-open, or the hangers get all mixed up, and then I start fixing that instead. But I know that’s not the real reason. I came in because I can’t make myself go near the front windows or that part of the street anymore, and it’s messing with my job because I’m supposed to be up there sometimes.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious when you think about the front windows or that part of the street when you're not actually there?\n\nPatient: Yeah… even when I’m not there, if I just think about the front windows or that part of the street, I get uncomfortable. Like, I’ll be at home or on the bus or something and it pops into my head, and then I start thinking about how I’m going to avoid it the next shift.\n\nAnd then I end up making these stupid little plans, like, okay, I’ll stay in the back and do returns, or I’ll sort boxes, or I’ll say I need to organize the stockroom. Which, I mean, those things do need doing—retail is kind of endless that way—but I know I’m really just trying not to go near the front. It’s part of my job, so it’s not like I can just never go there, but lately I kind of… can’t make myself.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still feel the need to avoid the front windows or that part of the street if you weren't alone?\n\nPatient: I think… yeah, I probably still would. Like, if someone was with me, maybe I’d feel a little less weird for a second, because I could talk or pretend we were just doing normal stuff, you know? But I don’t think it would really fix it.\n\nI mean, at work there are people around all the time—coworkers, customers, whatever—and I still keep finding reasons to stay in the back. I’ll suddenly be like, “Oh, I should sort these boxes,” or “I need to check the stockroom,” and then an hour goes by. And honestly, that’s part of why I came in, because I can’t make myself go near those front windows or that part of the street anymore, even though I’m supposed to for my job.\n\nSorry, I’m kind of going in circles. But yeah… being with someone might help a little, but I’d still want to avoid it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think something would happen to you if you didn't avoid the front windows or that part of the street?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I think something would happen, exactly. It’s not like I have a clear thought like, “If I stand there, this specific thing will happen.” It’s more… I just can’t make myself go there.\n\nLike even before my shift I’ll be thinking, okay, maybe I can just stay in the back and do the boxes, or fix the hangers, or say the stockroom needs sorting. And honestly it always does need sorting, so it’s easy to kind of hide behind that. My manager probably thinks I’m just being helpful or avoiding customers or something, but it’s not that.\n\nI came in because I can’t go near the front windows or that part of the street anymore, not like I’m supposed to. It’s part of my job, and I keep making excuses to stay in the back instead. So… I don’t know if I’d say I’m sure something would happen, but my body acts like I need to stay away.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. So… I don’t know if I’d say I’m sure something would happen, but my body acts like I need to stay away.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you think the front windows or that part of the street remind you of something unpleasant?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 23 / retail_salesperson / high_school\n- severity: Moderate\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Trauma_Avoidance\nmanifestation: \"I came in because I can’t make myself go near the front windows or that part of the street anymore, and I keep finding excuses to stay in the back even though that’s part of my job.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Tangent patient.\nA patient who goes off on tangents may\n1) begin answering the doctor's question but quickly shift to an unrelated topic,\n2) frequently add irrelevant personal anecdotes or experiences while answering the question, and\n3) have difficulty remaining focused on the doctor's question, potentially requiring the doctor to repeat the question or redirect the conversation to obtain clear clinical information.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… I think it does, or at least it feels like it does. I don’t always get a clear picture in my head, it’s more like when I see the front windows or even that stretch of street, my brain just goes, nope, don’t go there.

And then I start doing this whole thing where I suddenly become really interested in the back room, which is kind of funny because nobody is ever interested in the back room. It’s just boxes and hangers and those weird piles of clothes people leave when they change their mind. But I’ll be like, “Oh, I should stay back here and sort this,” or “I need to organize stock,” even though I know I’m just avoiding the front.

That’s basically why I came in. I can’t make myself go near the front windows or that part of the street anymore, and I keep finding excuses to stay in the back, even though going up front is part of my job.

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n11\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF40.2, F43.1\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Um… I guess I came because I can’t really make myself go near the front windows anymore. Or that part of the street outside. At work I keep making excuses to stay in the back, like I’ll say I need to organize stock or check boxes, which I mean, there’s always boxes in retail, so it doesn’t sound that weird… but it’s not really about the boxes. It’s just that being up front feels… I don’t know, I just can’t do it, and it’s part of my job, so it’s becoming a problem.\n\nDoctor: Is there something specific outside the front windows or on that part of the street that you're afraid of?\n\nPatient: I mean… not one thing exactly, like not a person standing there or anything. It’s just that area. The front windows, and that stretch of street right outside them, I just… I can’t make myself go over there anymore.\n\nAt work I keep finding reasons to stay in the back, like saying I need to sort inventory or look for something in the stockroom. Which, honestly, the stockroom is always a mess anyway—my manager keeps saying we need a better system, and nobody puts sizes where they go—but… yeah, I know that’s not really why I’m doing it. Being up front is part of my job, and I’m avoiding it.\n\nDoctor: Is there something that you think might happen to you if you were to go near the front windows or that part of the street?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I can explain it right. It’s not like I have a whole clear picture of, “this exact thing will happen.” It’s more like… if I go near the front windows or that part of the street, my whole body just says no. So I end up staying in the back and making up reasons, like inventory or boxes or whatever.\n\nAnd the stupid thing is, I’m actually good with customers usually. Like I can fold a whole table of shirts while talking to someone about sizes, but now if I’m supposed to be up front, I just start looking for a reason not to be there. It’s becoming a problem because that’s part of my job.\n\nDoctor: Do you think something bad would happen to you if you went near the front windows or that part of the street?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I think it in words like that, like “something bad is going to happen.” It’s more like I just… can’t go there. The front windows, that part of the street, I start looking for any excuse to stay in the back.\n\nLike I’ll suddenly decide the stockroom needs organizing, which it always does, honestly, because people just shove hangers anywhere, and then I can spend twenty minutes pretending that’s the urgent thing. But really I know I’m avoiding the front. And it’s a problem because being up there is literally part of my job.\n\nDoctor: Is there something specific that you're afraid of when you think about being near the front windows or that part of the street?\n\nPatient: Not specific, no… I mean, that’s kind of what makes me feel ridiculous when I try to explain it. I can’t point to one thing and say, “that’s what I’m scared of.” It’s just the front windows and that part of the street. I came in because I can’t make myself go near them anymore.\n\nAt work I’ll keep finding excuses to stay in the back, like there’s always something to do back there anyway—boxes, returns, clothes people shoved in the wrong place—but I know I’m using it as a reason not to go up front. And being near the windows is part of my job, so… yeah, it’s becoming a real problem.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would feel anxious or uncomfortable if you were near the front windows or that part of the street for a reason unrelated to your job?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, even if it wasn’t for work, I don’t think I could just casually stand there or walk by that part of the street. It’s not really about the job itself, I guess.\n\nAt work it’s just obvious because I keep making excuses to stay in the back—like, suddenly the back room is my whole life, and there’s always some pile of clothes or boxes or whatever to mess with. But the real thing is I came in because I can’t make myself go near those front windows or that spot on the street anymore. Even if I didn’t work there, I think I’d still want to avoid it.\n\nDoctor: Is there something about the front windows or that part of the street itself that you're trying to avoid, rather than what might happen there?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think it’s more that place itself, if that makes sense. Like the front windows and that stretch of street just feel like… I don’t know, like I hit a wall.\n\nI keep telling myself I’m just busy in the back, because there really is always stuff back there—someone leaves boxes half-open, or the hangers get all mixed up, and then I start fixing that instead. But I know that’s not the real reason. I came in because I can’t make myself go near the front windows or that part of the street anymore, and it’s messing with my job because I’m supposed to be up there sometimes.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious when you think about the front windows or that part of the street when you're not actually there?\n\nPatient: Yeah… even when I’m not there, if I just think about the front windows or that part of the street, I get uncomfortable. Like, I’ll be at home or on the bus or something and it pops into my head, and then I start thinking about how I’m going to avoid it the next shift.\n\nAnd then I end up making these stupid little plans, like, okay, I’ll stay in the back and do returns, or I’ll sort boxes, or I’ll say I need to organize the stockroom. Which, I mean, those things do need doing—retail is kind of endless that way—but I know I’m really just trying not to go near the front. It’s part of my job, so it’s not like I can just never go there, but lately I kind of… can’t make myself.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still feel the need to avoid the front windows or that part of the street if you weren't alone?\n\nPatient: I think… yeah, I probably still would. Like, if someone was with me, maybe I’d feel a little less weird for a second, because I could talk or pretend we were just doing normal stuff, you know? But I don’t think it would really fix it.\n\nI mean, at work there are people around all the time—coworkers, customers, whatever—and I still keep finding reasons to stay in the back. I’ll suddenly be like, “Oh, I should sort these boxes,” or “I need to check the stockroom,” and then an hour goes by. And honestly, that’s part of why I came in, because I can’t make myself go near those front windows or that part of the street anymore, even though I’m supposed to for my job.\n\nSorry, I’m kind of going in circles. But yeah… being with someone might help a little, but I’d still want to avoid it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think something would happen to you if you didn't avoid the front windows or that part of the street?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I think something would happen, exactly. It’s not like I have a clear thought like, “If I stand there, this specific thing will happen.” It’s more… I just can’t make myself go there.\n\nLike even before my shift I’ll be thinking, okay, maybe I can just stay in the back and do the boxes, or fix the hangers, or say the stockroom needs sorting. And honestly it always does need sorting, so it’s easy to kind of hide behind that. My manager probably thinks I’m just being helpful or avoiding customers or something, but it’s not that.\n\nI came in because I can’t go near the front windows or that part of the street anymore, not like I’m supposed to. It’s part of my job, and I keep making excuses to stay in the back instead. So… I don’t know if I’d say I’m sure something would happen, but my body acts like I need to stay away.\n\nDoctor: Do you think the front windows or that part of the street remind you of something unpleasant?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think it does, or at least it feels like it does. I don’t always get a clear picture in my head, it’s more like when I see the front windows or even that stretch of street, my brain just goes, nope, don’t go there.\n\nAnd then I start doing this whole thing where I suddenly become really interested in the back room, which is kind of funny because nobody is ever interested in the back room. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I mean… that’s kind of the thing, I guess. I came in because I can’t make myself go near the front windows or that part of the street anymore.

Like, I’ll be at work and suddenly I’m making all these excuses to stay in the back. “Oh, I should fold this,” or “I should check the stock,” or whatever. And the back room is always a mess anyway, so it’s easy to make it look like I’m doing something useful. But really I’m just trying not to go up there, even though being near the front is part of my job.

So… yeah, something about that area is upsetting enough that I keep avoiding it. I don’t know, I’m not explaining it very well.

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n12\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF40.2, F43.1\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Um… I guess I came because I can’t really make myself go near the front windows anymore. Or that part of the street outside. At work I keep making excuses to stay in the back, like I’ll say I need to organize stock or check boxes, which I mean, there’s always boxes in retail, so it doesn’t sound that weird… but it’s not really about the boxes. It’s just that being up front feels… I don’t know, I just can’t do it, and it’s part of my job, so it’s becoming a problem.\n\nDoctor: Is there something specific outside the front windows or on that part of the street that you're afraid of?\n\nPatient: I mean… not one thing exactly, like not a person standing there or anything. It’s just that area. The front windows, and that stretch of street right outside them, I just… I can’t make myself go over there anymore.\n\nAt work I keep finding reasons to stay in the back, like saying I need to sort inventory or look for something in the stockroom. Which, honestly, the stockroom is always a mess anyway—my manager keeps saying we need a better system, and nobody puts sizes where they go—but… yeah, I know that’s not really why I’m doing it. Being up front is part of my job, and I’m avoiding it.\n\nDoctor: Is there something that you think might happen to you if you were to go near the front windows or that part of the street?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I can explain it right. It’s not like I have a whole clear picture of, “this exact thing will happen.” It’s more like… if I go near the front windows or that part of the street, my whole body just says no. So I end up staying in the back and making up reasons, like inventory or boxes or whatever.\n\nAnd the stupid thing is, I’m actually good with customers usually. Like I can fold a whole table of shirts while talking to someone about sizes, but now if I’m supposed to be up front, I just start looking for a reason not to be there. It’s becoming a problem because that’s part of my job.\n\nDoctor: Do you think something bad would happen to you if you went near the front windows or that part of the street?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I think it in words like that, like “something bad is going to happen.” It’s more like I just… can’t go there. The front windows, that part of the street, I start looking for any excuse to stay in the back.\n\nLike I’ll suddenly decide the stockroom needs organizing, which it always does, honestly, because people just shove hangers anywhere, and then I can spend twenty minutes pretending that’s the urgent thing. But really I know I’m avoiding the front. And it’s a problem because being up there is literally part of my job.\n\nDoctor: Is there something specific that you're afraid of when you think about being near the front windows or that part of the street?\n\nPatient: Not specific, no… I mean, that’s kind of what makes me feel ridiculous when I try to explain it. I can’t point to one thing and say, “that’s what I’m scared of.” It’s just the front windows and that part of the street. I came in because I can’t make myself go near them anymore.\n\nAt work I’ll keep finding excuses to stay in the back, like there’s always something to do back there anyway—boxes, returns, clothes people shoved in the wrong place—but I know I’m using it as a reason not to go up front. And being near the windows is part of my job, so… yeah, it’s becoming a real problem.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would feel anxious or uncomfortable if you were near the front windows or that part of the street for a reason unrelated to your job?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, even if it wasn’t for work, I don’t think I could just casually stand there or walk by that part of the street. It’s not really about the job itself, I guess.\n\nAt work it’s just obvious because I keep making excuses to stay in the back—like, suddenly the back room is my whole life, and there’s always some pile of clothes or boxes or whatever to mess with. But the real thing is I came in because I can’t make myself go near those front windows or that spot on the street anymore. Even if I didn’t work there, I think I’d still want to avoid it.\n\nDoctor: Is there something about the front windows or that part of the street itself that you're trying to avoid, rather than what might happen there?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think it’s more that place itself, if that makes sense. Like the front windows and that stretch of street just feel like… I don’t know, like I hit a wall.\n\nI keep telling myself I’m just busy in the back, because there really is always stuff back there—someone leaves boxes half-open, or the hangers get all mixed up, and then I start fixing that instead. But I know that’s not the real reason. I came in because I can’t make myself go near the front windows or that part of the street anymore, and it’s messing with my job because I’m supposed to be up there sometimes.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious when you think about the front windows or that part of the street when you're not actually there?\n\nPatient: Yeah… even when I’m not there, if I just think about the front windows or that part of the street, I get uncomfortable. It’s part of my job, so it’s not like I can just never go there, but lately I kind of… can’t make myself.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still feel the need to avoid the front windows or that part of the street if you weren't alone?\n\nPatient: I think… yeah, I probably still would. I… I don’t know how to say yes without making it sound like I’m giving you this whole dramatic story, because I keep kind of circling around it. But yeah, something about there is bad enough that I can’t go near it now.

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n13\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF40.2, F43.1\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Um… I guess I came because I can’t really make myself go near the front windows anymore. Or that part of the street outside. At work I keep making excuses to stay in the back, like I’ll say I need to organize stock or check boxes, which I mean, there’s always boxes in retail, so it doesn’t sound that weird… but it’s not really about the boxes. It’s just that being up front feels… I don’t know, I just can’t do it, and it’s part of my job, so it’s becoming a problem.\n\nDoctor: Is there something specific outside the front windows or on that part of the street that you're afraid of?\n\nPatient: I mean… not one thing exactly, like not a person standing there or anything. It’s just that area. The front windows, and that stretch of street right outside them, I just… I can’t make myself go over there anymore.\n\nAt work I keep finding reasons to stay in the back, like saying I need to sort inventory or look for something in the stockroom. Which, honestly, the stockroom is always a mess anyway—my manager keeps saying we need a better system, and nobody puts sizes where they go—but… yeah, I know that’s not really why I’m doing it. Being up front is part of my job, and I’m avoiding it.\n\nDoctor: Is there something that you think might happen to you if you were to go near the front windows or that part of the street?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I can explain it right. It’s not like I have a whole clear picture of, “this exact thing will happen.” It’s more like… if I go near the front windows or that part of the street, my whole body just says no. So I end up staying in the back and making up reasons, like inventory or boxes or whatever.\n\nAnd the stupid thing is, I’m actually good with customers usually. Like I can fold a whole table of shirts while talking to someone about sizes, but now if I’m supposed to be up front, I just start looking for a reason not to be there. It’s becoming a problem because that’s part of my job.\n\nDoctor: Do you think something bad would happen to you if you went near the front windows or that part of the street?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I think it in words like that, like “something bad is going to happen.” It’s more like I just… can’t go there. The front windows, that part of the street, I start looking for any excuse to stay in the back.\n\nLike I’ll suddenly decide the stockroom needs organizing, which it always does, honestly, because people just shove hangers anywhere, and then I can spend twenty minutes pretending that’s the urgent thing. But really I know I’m avoiding the front. And it’s a problem because being up there is literally part of my job.\n\nDoctor: Is there something specific that you're afraid of when you think about being near the front windows or that part of the street?\n\nPatient: Not specific, no… I mean, that’s kind of what makes me feel ridiculous when I try to explain it. I can’t point to one thing and say, “that’s what I’m scared of.” It’s just the front windows and that part of the street. I came in because I can’t make myself go near them anymore.\n\nAt work I’ll keep finding excuses to stay in the back, like there’s always something to do back there anyway—boxes, returns, clothes people shoved in the wrong place—but I know I’m using it as a reason not to go up front. And being near the windows is part of my job, so… yeah, it’s becoming a real problem.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would feel anxious or uncomfortable if you were near the front windows or that part of the street for a reason unrelated to your job?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, even if it wasn’t for work, I don’t think I could just casually stand there or walk by that part of the street. It’s not really about the job itself, I guess.\n\nAt work it’s just obvious because I keep making excuses to stay in the back—like, suddenly the back room is my whole life, and there’s always some pile of clothes or boxes or whatever to mess with. But the real thing is I came in because I can’t make myself go near those front windows or that spot on the street anymore. Even if I didn’t work there, I think I’d still want to avoid it.\n\nDoctor: Is there something about the front windows or that part of the street itself that you're trying to avoid, rather than what might happen there?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think it’s more that place itself, if that makes sense. Like the front windows and that stretch of street just feel like… I don’t know, like I hit a wall.\n\nI keep telling myself I’m just busy in the back, because there really is always stuff back there—someone leaves boxes half-open, or the hangers get all mixed up, and then I start fixing that instead. But I know that’s not the real reason. I came in because I can’t make myself go near the front windows or that part of the street anymore, and it’s messing with my job because I’m supposed to be up there sometimes.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious when you think about the front windows or that part of the street when you're not actually there?\n\nPatient: Yeah… even when I’m not there, if I just think about the front windows or that part of the street, I get uncomfortable. Like, I’ll be at home or on the bus or something and it pops into my head, and then I start thinking about how I’m going to avoid it the next shift.\n\nAnd then I end up making these stupid little plans, like, okay, I’ll stay in the back and do returns, or I’ll sort boxes, or I’ll say I need to organize the stockroom. Which, I mean, those things do need doing—retail is kind of endless that way—but I know I’m really just trying not to go near the front. It’s part of my job, so it’s not like I can just never go there, but lately I kind of… can’t make myself.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still feel the need to avoid the front windows or that part of the street if you weren't alone?\n\nPatient: I think… yeah, I probably still would. Like, if someone was with me, maybe I’d feel a little less weird for a second, because I could talk or pretend we were just doing normal stuff, you know? But I don’t think it would really fix it.\n\nI mean, at work there are people around all the time—coworkers, customers, whatever—and I still keep finding reasons to stay in the back. I’ll suddenly be like, “Oh, I should sort these boxes,” or “I need to check the stockroom,” and then an hour goes by. And honestly, that’s part of why I came in, because I can’t make myself go near those front windows or that part of the street anymore, even though I’m supposed to for my job.\n\nSorry, I’m kind of going in circles. But yeah… being with someone might help a little, but I’d still want to avoid it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think something would happen to you if you didn't avoid the front windows or that part of the street?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I think something would happen, exactly. It’s not like I have a clear thought like, “If I stand there, this specific thing will happen.” It’s more… I just can’t make myself go there.\n\nLike even before my shift I’ll be thinking, okay, maybe I can just stay in the back and do the boxes, or fix the hangers, or say the stockroom needs sorting. And honestly it always does need sorting, so it’s easy to kind of hide behind that. My manager probably thinks I’m just being helpful or avoiding customers or something, but it’s not that.\n\nI came in because I can’t go near the front windows or that part of the street anymore, not like I’m supposed to. It’s part of my job, and I keep making excuses to stay in the back instead. So… I don’t know if I’d say I’m sure something would happen, but my body acts like I need to stay away.\n\nDoctor: Do you think the front windows or that part of the street remind you of something unpleasant?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think it does, or at least it feels like it does. I don’t always get a clear picture in my head, it’s more like when I see the front windows or even that stretch of street, my brain just goes, nope, don’t go there.\n\nAnd then I start doing this whole thing where I suddenly become really interested in the back room, which is kind of funny because nobody is ever interested in the back room. It’s just boxes and hangers and those weird piles of clothes people leave when they change their mind. But I’ll be like, “Oh, I should stay back here and sort this,” or “I need to organize stock,” even though I know I’m just avoiding the front.\n\nThat’s basically why I came in. I can’t make myself go near the front windows or that part of the street anymore, and I keep finding excuses to stay in the back, even though going up front is part of my job.\n\nDoctor: Did something upsetting happen to you near the front windows or that part of the street?\n\nPatient: I mean… that’s kind of the thing, I guess. I came in because I can’t make myself go near the front windows or that part of the street anymore.\n\nLike, I’ll be at work and suddenly I’m making all these excuses to stay in the back. “Oh, I should fold this,” or “I should check the stock,” or whatever. I don’t know, I’m not explaining it very well.\n\nDoctor: Did something upsetting or traumatic happen to you near the front windows or that part of the street that you're now avoiding?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t know how to say yes without making it sound like I’m giving you this whole dramatic story, because I keep kind of circling around it. At work I keep finding reasons to stay in the back, like sorting hangers or pretending the stockroom suddenly needs my full attention, which honestly it usually does, but that’s not really why I’m doing it. I came in because I can’t make myself go near the front windows or that part of the street anymore, and it’s messing with my job because I’m supposed to be up there sometimes.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious when you think about the front windows or that part of the street when you're not actually there?\n\nPatient: Yeah… even when I’m not there, if I just think about the front windows or that part of the street, I get uncomfortable. Like, I’ll be at home or on the bus or something and it pops into my head, and then I start thinking about how I’m going to avoid it the next shift.\n\nAnd then I end up making these stupid little plans, like, okay, I’ll stay in the back and do returns, or I’ll sort boxes, or I’ll say I need to organize the stockroom. Which, I mean, those things do need doing—retail is kind of endless that way—but I know I’m really just trying not to go near the front. It’s part of my job, so it’s not like I can just never go there, but lately I kind of… can’t make myself.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still feel the need to avoid the front windows or that part of the street if you weren't alone?\n\nPatient: I think… yeah, I probably still would. Like, if someone was with me, maybe I’d feel a little less weird for a second, because I could talk or pretend we were just doing normal stuff, you know? But I don’t think it would really fix it.\n\nI mean, at work there are people around all the time—coworkers, customers, whatever—and I still keep finding reasons to stay in the back. I’ll suddenly be like, “Oh, I should sort these boxes,” or “I need to check the stockroom,” and then an hour goes by. And honestly, that’s part of why I came in, because I can’t make myself go near those front windows or that part of the street anymore, even though I’m supposed to for my job.\n\nSorry, I’m kind of going in circles. But yeah… being with someone might help a little, but I’d still want to avoid it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think something would happen to you if you didn't avoid the front windows or that part of the street?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I think something would happen, exactly. It’s not like I have a clear thought like, “If I stand there, this specific thing will happen.” It’s more… I just can’t make myself go there.\n\nLike even before my shift I’ll be thinking, okay, maybe I can just stay in the back and do the boxes, or fix the hangers, or say the stockroom needs sorting. And honestly it always does need sorting, so it’s easy to kind of hide behind that. My manager probably thinks I’m just being helpful or avoiding customers or something, but it’s not that.\n\nI came in because I can’t go near the front windows or that part of the street anymore, not like I’m supposed to. So… I don’t know if I’d say I’m sure something would happen, but my body acts like I need to stay away.\n\nDoctor: Do you think the front windows or that part of the street remind you of something unpleasant?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think it does, or at least it feels like it does. I don’t always get a clear picture in my head, it’s more like when I see the front windows or even that stretch of street, my brain just goes, nope, don’t go there.\n\nAnd then I start doing this whole thing where I suddenly become really interested in the back room, which is kind of funny because nobody is ever interested in the back room. It’s just boxes and hangers and those weird piles of clothes people leave when they change their mind. I can’t make myself go near the front windows or that part of the street anymore, and I keep finding excuses to stay in the back, even though going up front is part of my job.\n\nDoctor: Did something upsetting happen to you near the front windows or that part of the street?\n\nPatient: I mean… that’s kind of the thing, I guess. I don’t know, I’m not explaining it very well.\n\nDoctor: Did something upsetting or traumatic happen to you near the front windows or that part of the street that you're now avoiding?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t know how to say yes without making it sound like I’m giving you this whole dramatic story, because I keep kind of circling around it. At work I keep finding reasons to stay in the back, like sorting hangers or pretending the stockroom suddenly needs my full attention, which honestly it usually does, but that’s not really why I’m doing it. I came in because I can’t make myself go near them anymore.\n\nAt work I’ll keep finding excuses to stay in the back, like there’s always something to do back there anyway—boxes, returns, clothes people shoved in the wrong place—but I know I’m using it as a reason not to go up front. And being near the windows is part of my job, so… yeah, it’s becoming a real problem.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would feel anxious or uncomfortable if you were near the front windows or that part of the street for a reason unrelated to your job?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, even if it wasn’t for work, I don’t think I could just casually stand there or walk by that part of the street. It’s not really about the job itself, I guess.\n\nAt work it’s just obvious because I keep making excuses to stay in the back—like, suddenly the back room is my whole life, and there’s always some pile of clothes or boxes or whatever to mess with. But the real thing is I came in because I can’t make myself go near those front windows or that spot on the street anymore. Even if I didn’t work there, I think I’d still want to avoid it.\n\nDoctor: Is there something about the front windows or that part of the street itself that you're trying to avoid, rather than what might happen there?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think it’s more that place itself, if that makes sense. Like the front windows and that stretch of street just feel like… I don’t know, like I hit a wall.\n\nI keep telling myself I’m just busy in the back, because there really is always stuff back there—someone leaves boxes half-open, or the hangers get all mixed up, and then I start fixing that instead. But I know that’s not the real reason. I came in because I can’t make myself go near the front windows or that part of the street anymore, and it’s messing with my job because I’m supposed to be up there sometimes.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious when you think about the front windows or that part of the street when you're not actually there?\n\nPatient: Yeah… even when I’m not there, if I just think about the front windows or that part of the street, I get uncomfortable. Like, I’ll be at home or on the bus or something and it pops into my head, and then I start thinking about how I’m going to avoid it the next shift.\n\nAnd then I end up making these stupid little plans, like, okay, I’ll stay in the back and do returns, or I’ll sort boxes, or I’ll say I need to organize the stockroom. Which, I mean, those things do need doing—retail is kind of endless that way—but I know I’m really just trying not to go near the front. It’s part of my job, so it’s not like I can just never go there, but lately I kind of… can’t make myself.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still feel the need to avoid the front windows or that part of the street if you weren't alone?\n\nPatient: I think… yeah, I probably still would. Like, if someone was with me, maybe I’d feel a little less weird for a second, because I could talk or pretend we were just doing normal stuff, you know? But I don’t think it would really fix it.\n\nI mean, at work there are people around all the time—coworkers, customers, whatever—and I still keep finding reasons to stay in the back. I’ll suddenly be like, “Oh, I should sort these boxes,” or “I need to check the stockroom,” and then an hour goes by. And honestly, that’s part of why I came in, because I can’t make myself go near those front windows or that part of the street anymore, even though I’m supposed to for my job.\n\nSorry, I’m kind of going in circles. But yeah… being with someone might help a little, but I’d still want to avoid it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think something would happen to you if you didn't avoid the front windows or that part of the street?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I think something would happen, exactly. It’s not like I have a clear thought like, “If I stand there, this specific thing will happen.” It’s more… I just can’t make myself go there.\n\nLike even before my shift I’ll be thinking, okay, maybe I can just stay in the back and do the boxes, or fix the hangers, or say the stockroom needs sorting. And honestly it always does need sorting, so it’s easy to kind of hide behind that. My manager probably thinks I’m just being helpful or avoiding customers or something, but it’s not that.\n\nI came in because I can’t go near the front windows or that part of the street anymore, not like I’m supposed to. It’s part of my job, and I keep making excuses to stay in the back instead. So… I don’t know if I’d say I’m sure something would happen, but my body acts like I need to stay away.\n\nDoctor: Do you think the front windows or that part of the street remind you of something unpleasant?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think it does, or at least it feels like it does. I don’t always get a clear picture in my head, it’s more like when I see the front windows or even that stretch of street, my brain just goes, nope, don’t go there.\n\nAnd then I start doing this whole thing where I suddenly become really interested in the back room, which is kind of funny because nobody is ever interested in the back room. It’s just boxes and hangers and those weird piles of clothes people leave when they change their mind. But I’ll be like, “Oh, I should stay back here and sort this,” or “I need to organize stock,” even though I know I’m just avoiding the front.\n\nThat’s basically why I came in. I can’t make myself go near the front windows or that part of the street anymore, and I keep finding excuses to stay in the back, even though going up front is part of my job.\n\nDoctor: Did something upsetting happen to you near the front windows or that part of the street?\n\nPatient: I mean… that’s kind of the thing, I guess. I came in because I can’t make myself go near the front windows or that part of the street anymore.\n\nLike, I’ll be at work and suddenly I’m making all these excuses to stay in the back. “Oh, I should fold this,” or “I should check the stock,” or whatever. I don’t know, I’m not explaining it very well.\n\nDoctor: Did something upsetting or traumatic happen to you near the front windows or that part of the street that you're now avoiding?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t know how to say yes without making it sound like I’m giving you this whole dramatic story, because I keep kind of circling around it. But yeah, something about there is bad enough that I can’t go near it now.\n\nI came in because I can’t make myself go by the front windows or that part of the street anymore. At work I keep finding reasons to stay in the back, like sorting hangers or pretending the stockroom suddenly needs my full attention, which honestly it usually does, but that’s not really why I’m doing it. It’s just easier to stay back there than go up front, even though being up front is part of my job.\n\nDoctor: Did something traumatic happen to you near the front windows or that part of the street?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I mean, I keep wanting to say yes because obviously something about it is affecting me, but when you ask it that directly, my brain kind of blanks.\n\nIt’s like when someone asks you where you put your keys and suddenly you can picture every purse you’ve ever owned but not the actual keys. Sorry, that’s not really what you asked. I just… I know I can’t go near that area without feeling like I have to get away, and I keep making excuses at work to avoid it.\n\nBut saying exactly what happened there… I’m not sure I can give you that clearly right now.\n\nDoctor: Did something traumatic happen to you near the front windows or that part of the street that you're now avoiding?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I think that’s why I’m here, because I can’t make myself go near the front windows or that part of the street anymore.\n\nAt work I’ll suddenly be like, “Oh, I need to go fold stuff in the back,” or “I should check inventory,” and I know it probably looks like I’m just trying to avoid customers or something, which is embarrassing because that’s literally my job. At work I keep finding reasons to stay in the back, like sorting hangers or pretending the stockroom suddenly needs my full attention, which honestly it usually does, but that’s not really why I’m doing it. It’s just easier to stay back there than go up front, even though being up front is part of my job.\n\nDoctor: Did something traumatic happen to you near the front windows or that part of the street?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I mean, I keep wanting to say yes because obviously something about it is affecting me, but when you ask it that directly, my brain kind of blanks.\n\nIt’s like when someone asks you where you put your keys and suddenly you can picture every purse you’ve ever owned but not the actual keys. Sorry, that’s not really what you asked. I just… I know I can’t go near that area without feeling like I have to get away, and I keep making excuses at work to avoid it.\n\nBut saying exactly what happened there… I’m not sure I can give you that clearly right now.\n\nDoctor: Did something traumatic happen to you near the front windows or that part of the street that you're now avoiding?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I think that’s why I’m here, because I can’t make myself go near the front windows or that part of the street anymore.\n\nAt work I’ll suddenly be like, “Oh, I need to go fold stuff in the back,” or “I should check inventory,” and I know it probably looks like I’m just trying to avoid customers or something, which is embarrassing because that’s literally my job. I used to be able to go up there without thinking about it. Now I keep finding excuses to stay away from that whole front area.\n\nSorry, I know I’m not giving you the exact answer you’re asking for. It’s just… yes, something about there is making me avoid it.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you remember what happened near the front windows or that part of the street that's causing you to avoid it?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 23 / retail_salesperson / high_school\n- severity: Moderate\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Intrusive_Symptoms\nmanifestation: \"The crash keeps popping into my head out of nowhere, especially when I’m trying to set up displays, and suddenly I can hear the bang and see the glass coming down like it’s happening again.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Tangent patient.\nA patient who goes off on tangents may\n1) begin answering the doctor's question but quickly shift to an unrelated topic,\n2) frequently add irrelevant personal anecdotes or experiences while answering the question, and\n3) have difficulty remaining focused on the doctor's question, potentially requiring the doctor to repeat the question or redirect the conversation to obtain clear clinical information.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I do remember parts of it, yeah. It’s not like a normal memory, though, where I can sit down and tell it in order.

It just pops into my head out of nowhere, especially when I’m trying to set up displays near the front. I’ll be moving shirts around or trying to make something look nice, and then suddenly I can hear that bang again, and I see the glass coming down like it’s happening right then. And then I’m not really thinking about the display anymore, obviously. It’s just… there.

  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n16\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF40.2, F43.1\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Um… I guess I came because I can’t really make myself go near the front windows anymore. Or that part of the street outside. At work I keep making excuses to stay in the back, like I’ll say I need to organize stock or check boxes, which I mean, there’s always boxes in retail, so it doesn’t sound that weird… but it’s not really about the boxes. It’s just that being up front feels… I don’t know, I just can’t do it, and it’s part of my job, so it’s becoming a problem.\n\nDoctor: Is there something specific outside the front windows or on that part of the street that you're afraid of?\n\nPatient: I mean… not one thing exactly, like not a person standing there or anything. It’s just that area. The front windows, and that stretch of street right outside them, I just… I can’t make myself go over there anymore.\n\nAt work I keep finding reasons to stay in the back, like saying I need to sort inventory or look for something in the stockroom. Which, honestly, the stockroom is always a mess anyway—my manager keeps saying we need a better system, and nobody puts sizes where they go—but… yeah, I know that’s not really why I’m doing it. Being up front is part of my job, and I’m avoiding it.\n\nDoctor: Is there something that you think might happen to you if you were to go near the front windows or that part of the street?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I can explain it right. It’s not like I have a whole clear picture of, “this exact thing will happen.” It’s more like… if I go near the front windows or that part of the street, my whole body just says no. So I end up staying in the back and making up reasons, like inventory or boxes or whatever.\n\nAnd the stupid thing is, I’m actually good with customers usually. Like I can fold a whole table of shirts while talking to someone about sizes, but now if I’m supposed to be up front, I just start looking for a reason not to be there. It’s becoming a problem because that’s part of my job.\n\nDoctor: Do you think something bad would happen to you if you went near the front windows or that part of the street?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I think it in words like that, like “something bad is going to happen.” It’s more like I just… can’t go there. The front windows, that part of the street, I start looking for any excuse to stay in the back.\n\nLike I’ll suddenly decide the stockroom needs organizing, which it always does, honestly, because people just shove hangers anywhere, and then I can spend twenty minutes pretending that’s the urgent thing. But really I know I’m avoiding the front. And it’s a problem because being up there is literally part of my job.\n\nDoctor: Is there something specific that you're afraid of when you think about being near the front windows or that part of the street?\n\nPatient: Not specific, no… I mean, that’s kind of what makes me feel ridiculous when I try to explain it. I can’t point to one thing and say, “that’s what I’m scared of.” It’s just the front windows and that part of the street. I came in because I can’t make myself go near them anymore.\n\nAt work I’ll keep finding excuses to stay in the back, like there’s always something to do back there anyway—boxes, returns, clothes people shoved in the wrong place—but I know I’m using it as a reason not to go up front. And being near the windows is part of my job, so… yeah, it’s becoming a real problem.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would feel anxious or uncomfortable if you were near the front windows or that part of the street for a reason unrelated to your job?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, even if it wasn’t for work, I don’t think I could just casually stand there or walk by that part of the street. It’s not really about the job itself, I guess.\n\nAt work it’s just obvious because I keep making excuses to stay in the back—like, suddenly the back room is my whole life, and there’s always some pile of clothes or boxes or whatever to mess with. But the real thing is I came in because I can’t make myself go near those front windows or that spot on the street anymore. Even if I didn’t work there, I think I’d still want to avoid it.\n\nDoctor: Is there something about the front windows or that part of the street itself that you're trying to avoid, rather than what might happen there?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think it’s more that place itself, if that makes sense. Like the front windows and that stretch of street just feel like… I don’t know, like I hit a wall.\n\nI keep telling myself I’m just busy in the back, because there really is always stuff back there—someone leaves boxes half-open, or the hangers get all mixed up, and then I start fixing that instead. But I know that’s not the real reason. I came in because I can’t make myself go near the front windows or that part of the street anymore, and it’s messing with my job because I’m supposed to be up there sometimes.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious when you think about the front windows or that part of the street when you're not actually there?\n\nPatient: Yeah… even when I’m not there, if I just think about the front windows or that part of the street, I get uncomfortable. Like, I’ll be at home or on the bus or something and it pops into my head, and then I start thinking about how I’m going to avoid it the next shift.\n\nAnd then I end up making these stupid little plans, like, okay, I’ll stay in the back and do returns, or I’ll sort boxes, or I’ll say I need to organize the stockroom. Which, I mean, those things do need doing—retail is kind of endless that way—but I know I’m really just trying not to go near the front. It’s part of my job, so it’s not like I can just never go there, but lately I kind of… can’t make myself.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still feel the need to avoid the front windows or that part of the street if you weren't alone?\n\nPatient: I think… yeah, I probably still would. Like, if someone was with me, maybe I’d feel a little less weird for a second, because I could talk or pretend we were just doing normal stuff, you know? But I don’t think it would really fix it.\n\nI mean, at work there are people around all the time—coworkers, customers, whatever—and I still keep finding reasons to stay in the back. I’ll suddenly be like, “Oh, I should sort these boxes,” or “I need to check the stockroom,” and then an hour goes by. And honestly, that’s part of why I came in, because I can’t make myself go near those front windows or that part of the street anymore, even though I’m supposed to for my job.\n\nSorry, I’m kind of going in circles. But yeah… being with someone might help a little, but I’d still want to avoid it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think something would happen to you if you didn't avoid the front windows or that part of the street?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I think something would happen, exactly. It’s not like I have a clear thought like, “If I stand there, this specific thing will happen.” It’s more… I just can’t make myself go there.\n\nLike even before my shift I’ll be thinking, okay, maybe I can just stay in the back and do the boxes, or fix the hangers, or say the stockroom needs sorting. And honestly it always does need sorting, so it’s easy to kind of hide behind that. My manager probably thinks I’m just being helpful or avoiding customers or something, but it’s not that.\n\nI came in because I can’t go near the front windows or that part of the street anymore, not like I’m supposed to. It’s part of my job, and I keep making excuses to stay in the back instead. So… I don’t know if I’d say I’m sure something would happen, but my body acts like I need to stay away.\n\nDoctor: Do you think the front windows or that part of the street remind you of something unpleasant?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think it does, or at least it feels like it does. I don’t always get a clear picture in my head, it’s more like when I see the front windows or even that stretch of street, my brain just goes, nope, don’t go there.\n\nAnd then I start doing this whole thing where I suddenly become really interested in the back room, which is kind of funny because nobody is ever interested in the back room. It’s just boxes and hangers and those weird piles of clothes people leave when they change their mind. But I’ll be like, “Oh, I should stay back here and sort this,” or “I need to organize stock,” even though I know I’m just avoiding the front.\n\nThat’s basically why I came in. I can’t make myself go near the front windows or that part of the street anymore, and I keep finding excuses to stay in the back, even though going up front is part of my job.\n\nDoctor: Did something upsetting happen to you near the front windows or that part of the street?\n\nPatient: I mean… that’s kind of the thing, I guess. I came in because I can’t make myself go near the front windows or that part of the street anymore.\n\nLike, I’ll be at work and suddenly I’m making all these excuses to stay in the back. “Oh, I should fold this,” or “I should check the stock,” or whatever. And the back room is always a mess anyway, so it’s easy to make it look like I’m doing something useful. But really I’m just trying not to go up there, even though being near the front is part of my job.\n\nSo… yeah, something about that area is upsetting enough that I keep avoiding it. I don’t know, I’m not explaining it very well.\n\nDoctor: Did something upsetting or traumatic happen to you near the front windows or that part of the street that you're now avoiding?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t know how to say yes without making it sound like I’m giving you this whole dramatic story, because I keep kind of circling around it.
